THE GREAT SPECIFIC ISSUE BEFORE EISENHOWER AND CHURCHILL IS WHAT, 
IF ANYTHING, THEIR NATIONS ARE GOING TO DO ABOUT BLOCKING COMMUNIST 
CONQUEST IN SOUTHEAST ASIA. EDEN'S SPEECH WAS READ HERE AS ALMOST 
SOUNDING THE DEATH KNELL OF ANY REAL AGREEMENT. 

EISENHOWER AND DULLES WANT QUICK BRITISH COOPERATION IN STEPS TO 
FORM AN ANTI-COMMUNIST ALLIANCE IN SOUTHEAST ASIA~e EDEN SPOKE OF THE 
POSSISILITY OF SUCH A DEFENSE PACT, SUT HE rater. x THE CHANCE 
THAT IT WOULD DO ANY GOOD IN THE IMMEDIATE FUTURE AND HE COUPLED IT 
WITH 4 SUGGESTION FOR LOCARNO-LIKE NON-AGGRESSION PACTS, PRESUMABLY 
INCLUDING BOTH RED CHINA AND THE UNITED STATES. 

THE UNITED STATES COLD-SHOULDERED THIS SUGGESTION. OFFICIALS SuUS- 
PECTED IT WAS MOTIVATED (1) BY.A DESIRE ON EDEN'S PART TO SHOW 
INDEPENDENCE OF AMERICAN POLICY IDEAS 9 AND (2) TO PLACE ANOTHER 08- 
STACLE IN ThE WAY OF ANY QUICK AGREEMENT ON AN ASIAN PACT. 

BRITISH DIPLOMATS, ON THE OTHER HAND, SAID THE EDE PROPOSAL WA 


BRITISH DIPLOMATS, ON THE OTHER HAND, SAID THE EDEN PROPOSAL WAS 
PUT FORWARD IN ALL SERIOUSNESS AS A PRACTICAL WAY OF OBTAINING SUPPORT 
FOR AN ASIAN SECURITY SYSTEM OF BRITISH COMMONWEALTH COUNTRIES LIKE 


INDIA ND CEYLON WHICH TEND TO BE "NEUTRALISTS" IN THEIR

JUNE 25 (AP)-SE (R-SD) SAID TODAY THE LAW SHOULD 


WASHINGTON 
BE ~CHAt LET ANY SENATE COMMITTEE “TAKE CARE OF A RECALCITRANT

A SIMELAR VIEW WAS VOICED BY SEN. DWORSHAK (R-IDAHO). IN SEPARATE 
INTERVIEWS THEY EMPHASIZED THEIR REMARKS WERE NOT AIMED AT SEN. MC- 
CARTHY (R-WIS), CHAIRMAN OF THE GOVERNMENT OPERATIONS COMMITTEE AND ITS 
PERMANENT INVESTIGATIONS SUBCOMMITTEE. 

MUNDT, A FRIEND OF MCCARTHY, IS THE MAN WHO PRESIDED AT THE RECENT 
MCCARTHY*ARMY HEARINGS BY THE SUBCOMMITTEE. DWORSHAK, A GOVERNMENT 
NPERATIONS COMMITTEE MEMBER, SERVED TEMPORARILY ON THE SUBCOMMITTEE IN 
MCCARTHY'S PLACE FOR THOSE HEARINGS. 

MUNDT SAID THE GOVERNMENT REORGANIZATION LAW HAS "A BIG BLIND SPOT® 
IN THAT IT PROVIDES NO MACHINERY BY WHICH MEMBERS OF ANY SENATE COM- 
MITTEE CAN CALL THEMSELVES INTO AN OFFICIAL MEETING WITHOUT THEIR CHAIR- 
MAN'S CONSENT, OR COMPEL THE CHAIRMAN TO CALL 4 MEETING. 

MUNDT SAID HE HAS BEEN MADE ACUTELY AWARE OF THIS BY PROBLEMS WHICH 
AROSE BEFORE AND SINCE THE MCCARTHY-ARMY HEARINGS. 

IN HIS HANDS NOW IS A SEALED REPLY FROM THE PENTAGON TC HIS REQUEST 
FoR A REPORT ON WHETHER TWO MEMBERS OF THE INVESTIGATIONS SUBCOMMITTEE 
STAFF HAVE EEN REFUSED CLEARANCE BY THE DEFENSE DEPARTMENT TO EXAMINE 

FCRET DOCUMENTS. 

SECON CLOSING HOURS OF THE HEARINGS ON MCCARTHY'S ROW WITH HIGH ARMY 
OFFICIALS, THE DEFENSE DEPARTMENT SUBMITTED A REPORT SHOWING, MUNDT 
SAID, *NQ CLEARANCE” FOR TWO INVESTIGATIONS SUBCOMMITTEE STAFF AIDES. 

MUNDT THEN ASKED THE PENTAGON TO BE MORE SPECIFIC--TO SAY WHETHER THIS 
MZANS THE TWO ARE DENIED CLEARANCE AS SECURITY RISKS OR WHETHER IT MERELY 
MEANS THAT THE DEFENSE DEPARTMENT IN MORE THAN A YEAR HAS NOT GOT 
MOUND TO MAKING A RULING ON THE TWO MEN'S APPLICATIONS. 

THE TWO EMPLOYES' HAVE NOT SEEN MADE PUBLIC OFFICIALLY. 

MUNDT RECEIVED THE PENTAGON'S REPLY LAST NIGHT AS CHAIRMAN OF THE 
MCCARTHY-ARMY HEARINGS SUBCOMMITTEE. HE SAID HE LOCKED IT IN HIS SAFE 
WREAD, UNTIL THE HEARINGS GROUP CAN MEET, PROBABLY MONDAY. 

IN MCCARTHY'S ABSENCE ON A VACATION AT SOME UNDISCLOSED SPOT, MUNDT 
SAID HE HAS NO AUTHORITY TO CALL A MEETING OF THE REGULAR INVESTIGA-

CyI.CA GO JUNE 25 (AP)-THE UNITED STATES, SAYS FORMER PRESIDENT 
HOOV NNOT RELAY ON MILITARY ALLIANCES OR "COLLECTIVE ACTION® 
FOR PERMANENT SECURITY. BECAUSE SHIFTING INTERESTS CAUSE 4 .CON- 

TINUOUS REALIGNMENT OF FORCES. 

"OUR PEOPLE SHOULD REABLZE,® HOOVER SAID LAST NIGHT, "THAT WITH 
THESE ALLIANCES, GROUPINGS, AND SHIFTINGS, THE WHOLE CONCEPT OF 
PRESERVATION OF PEACE BY COLLECTIVE ACTION THROUGH THE UNITED NATIONS 
HAS GONE BY THE BOARD, AT LEAST FOR THE PRESENT. f 

SPEAKING AT THE ANNUAL DINNER OF THE MERCHANDISE MART HALL OF FAME, 
HOOVER SAID “WE SHOULD NOT CONCLUDE THAT THE UNITED NATIONS-StovLD— 
BE ASOLISHED. X X X IT HAS CONTRIBUTED TO THE SPREAD OF SCIENTIFIC 
KNOWLEDGE, TO PHILANTHROPIC AND PUBLIC HEALTH MEASURES. 

"THESE ACTIVITIES FORM 4 TENUOUS ROAD TO MORE UNITY OF FREE NATIONS 
AND WE SHOULD CLING TO ANY HOPE THEY MAY PROVIDE. 

“ABOVE ALL, WE CANNOT ABANDON THE IDEAL THAT SOME DAY, SOMEHON, 
UNITY FOR PEACE CAN BE BUILT IN THE WORLD. NOR CAN WE ABANDON THE HOPE 
THAT SOME TIME THE FREE NATIONS WHO BELIEVE IN GOD WILL MOSILIZE 
AGAINST RED ATHEISM AND HUMAN SLAVERY. 7 

"IF INSIDE OR OUTSIDE OF THE UNITED NATIONS THE FREE NATIONS COULD 
UNITE, THEY HAVE MANY POTENT MORAL, SPIRITUAL AND EVEN ECONOMIC 
WEAPONS AT THEIR DISPOSAL BESIDES MILITARY ALLIANCES. ; 

THE FORMER PRESIDENT, SPEAKING TO SOME 500 FINANCIERS, MERCHANTS AND 
INDUSTRIALISTS, SAID THAT "OUR DIFFICULTIES ARE NOT LIMITED TO THE 
COMMUNISTS--THEY ARISE ALSO FROM A LACK OF UNITY AMONG THE FREE 
NATIONS IN DEALING WITH THESE DIFFICULTI=S."® 

®WE MUST REALIZE,® HE ADDED, "THAT THERE ARE WIDE DIFFERENCES IN 
RACIAL INHERITANCES, ECONOMIC AND POLITICAL OBJECTIVES IN EACH OF 
THE FREE NATIONS. X X X IN DEALING WITH THESE GIGANTIC PROBLEMS OF 
TODAY WE MUST HAVE PATIENCE, TOLERANCE AND UNDERSTANDING OF THESE 
DIFFERENCES OF INTERESTS AND OBJECTIVES OF THE FREE NATIONS." 

DECLARING THAT "THROUGH THIS TERRIBLE LABYRINTH OUR GOVERNMENT MUST 
PILOT OUR FOREIGN POLICIES," HOOVER SAID “WE CANNOT EXPECT MIRACLES.® 


HOOVER SAID NATIONALISM -- CONSIDERING THE WELFARE OF ONE'S 
OWN COUNTRY FIRST -- IS 4 “GREAT SPIRITUAL FORCE” THAT WILL HELP KEEP 
metiaer’ FREE AND DOES NOT MEAN ISOLATIONISM FROM COMMON INTERESTS OF

Y THEY HAVE ASKED THE UNITED STATES IF IT IS 
GOING TO DRAW A LINE IN SOUTHEAST ASIA, AND IF SO WHERE. THE 
CAMBODIANS SAID THEY WANT TO KNOW ALSO’ WHETHER THE UNITED STATES 
WILL PROTECT THEIR INDEPENDENCE, OR WHETHER THEY MUST GO IT ALONG 
AND MAKE THE BEST DEAL POSSIBLE WITH RED CHINA} 

CAMBODIAN SOURCES SAY THESE ISSUES, GROWING FROM CURRENT TALKS oN 
THE WITHDRAWAL OF COMMUNIST-LED VIETMINH TROOPS FROM CAMBODIAN SOIL, 
WERE PUT TO AMERICAN OFFICIALS ON THE EVE OF PRESIDENT EISENHOWER'S 
CONFERENCES WITH BRITISH PRIME MINISTER CHURCHILL ON POLICIES IN 
SOUTHEAST ASIA 

THE QUESTION WAS TRIGGERED BY RED CHINESE PREMIER-FOREIGN MINISTER 
CHOU EN-LAI IN HIS MEETING WITH CAMBODIAN OFFICIALS HERE LAST 
SATURDAY A FEW HOURS AFTER CHOU HAD SUDDENLY AGREED IN A CONFERENCE 
SESSION TO SEPARATE THE PROBLEMS OF LAOS AND CAMBODIA FROM THAT OF 
WARTORN VIET NAM. 

CHOU, FLANKED BY PHAM VAN DONG, VICE PREMIER OF THE VIETMINH REGIME, 
PROMISED, ACCORDING TO CAMBODIAN SOURCES, TO HAVE VIETMINH TROOPS 
PULLED OUT OF CAMBODIA, AND TO RECOGNIZE CAMBODIA'S INDEPENDENCE AND 
TERRITORIAL INTEGRITY ON CONDITION CAMBODIA REMAIN ALOOF FROM ANY 
*MEASURES LIKELY TO BE CONSIDERED AS A MENACE TO NEIGHBORING 

THIS PHRASE WAS INTERPRETED ON BOTH SIDES TO MEAN THEY WOULD 
THINK THIS OVER. IT IS STILL BEING EXAMINED PENDING SOME ANSWER 
FROM WASHINGTON. MEANWHILE, THE PROJECTED CAMBODIAN-VIETMINH TALKS 
FOR A COMMUNIST PULLBACK ARE BEING HELD IN ABEYANCE. 

CHOU'S PROPOSAL FOR THE NEUTRALIZATION OF CAMBODIA WAS 
FAVORABLY RECEIVED BY THE FRENCH ‘PREMIER PIERRE MENDES-FRANCE IN HIS 
TALK WITH THE RED CHINESE LEADER EARLIER THIS WEEK IN BERN. PRESUMABLY, 
CHOU IS NOW ADVANCING THE SAME PROJECT TO PRIME MINISTER NEHRU IN NEW

NEUTRALIZATION WOULD LEAVE THE COUNTRY DEFENSELESS AGAINST ANY 
POSSIBLE FUTURE COMMUNIST AGGRESSION.

ROC. CALL Ras JUNE 2S.(AP)-MAJs CHARLES E. YEAGER, KNOWN AS THE 
: PRVELING HUMAN, WAS PRESENTED THE DISTINGUISHED SERVICE 

MEDAL TODAY AT EDWARDS AIR FORCE BASE NEAR HERE. 

ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF AIR ROGER LEWIS PINNED THE MEDAL ON MAJ. 
YEAGER AS GEN. EDWIN RAWLINGS, CHIEF OF THE AIR MATERIEL COMMAND 
GEN. THOMAS S$. POWER, COMMANDER, AIR RESEARCH AND DEVELOPMENT COMMAND, . 
AND MEMBERS OF YEAGER'S FAMILY LOOKED ON. 

DURING AN UNCONTROLLED DIVE AT 70,000 FEET ON DEC. 12, 1953, YEAGER 
REACHED AN ESTIMATED 1,650 MePsHe INA BELL X-1A ROCKET RESEARCH PLANE. 
HE REPORTED THE SPEED KNOCKED HIM ABOUT IN THE COCKPIT. HE BROUGHT 
THE PLANE UNDER CONTROL AT 20,000 FEET. YEAGER FIRST SROKE THE SONIC 
BARRIER IN 1947s

WITNESSED THE DECORATION. OTHERS PRESENT WERE LT. GEN. DONALD L.

NER, COMMANDER OF EDWARDS AIR FORCE FLIGHT

EI GHT- YEAR- LONG INDOCHINA CONFLICT MARKED TIME TODAY, AWAITING SOME 
WORD ON HIGH LEVEL TALKS IN WASHINGTON AND NEW DELHI.

IN THE AMERICAN CAPITAL, WHILE INDIAN PRIME MINISTER NEHRU AND RED 
CHINESE PREMIER-FOREIGN MINISTER CHOU EN-LAI WERE CONFERRING IN NEW 


DELHI.» 


IN THE SOUTHEAST ASIA REGION, INCLUDING INDOCHINA, WHICH THE GENEVA 
CONFERENCE HAS BEEN DEBATING. MOST DELEGATION OFFICIALS FELT IT wAS 
POINTLESS TO PROCEED WITH THE WORK HERE UNTIL AMERICAN, BRITISH, 
INDIAN AND COMMUNIST CHINESE POLICIES ARE CLARIFIFD.

THE FIVE PRINCIPLES OF THAT PACT HE ENUMERATED AS$ MUTUAL RESPECT

NON-AGGRESSION, MUTUAL NON-INTERFERENCE IN EACH OTHER'S INTERNAL 
AFFAIRS, EQUALITY AND MUTUAL BENEFIT AND PEACEFUL CO-EXISTENCE. |

AS ®THE TIBETAN REGION OF CHINA.”

(EDITOR'S NOTE--WILLIAM JeWAUGH OF THE ASSOCIATED PRESS SPENT 


SEVERAL WEEKS IN THE MARSHALL ISLANDS, WHERE THE UNITED STATES TESTS 
ITS ATOMIC BOMBS. HE PREVIOUSLY REPORTED THE STORY OF THE MARSHALLESE 
SHOWERED UNEXPECTEDLY WITH RADIOACTIVE ASH AFTER THE MARCH 1 TEST OF 
THE HYDROGEN BOMB. TODAY HE TELLS OF THE TROUBLES OF THE NATIVES 


WHO HAD TO LEAVE BIKINI.) 


BY WILLIAM JeWAUGH | 
BEET eA RTTAWe ISLANDS (AP)-THIS [S THE “ISLAND OF HUNGRY PEOPLE.® 


THE INHABITANTS ARE BIKINIANS--200 MARSHALLESE WHOSE HOMF A 
BIKINI WAS TAKEN OVER EIGHT YEARS AGO FOR TESTING OF ATOMIC WEAPONS. 

"WHEN BIG WAVES COME IN WE SAY KILI JUST LIKE CALABOOSE,” SAID 
THEIVER SINCE THEY LEFT HOME, THE LEADER SAID. Keg BE 

E ID, HIS PEOPLE 

"PROMISES VANISH." ‘ ’ OPLE HAVE SEEN 

*OUR’ CLOTHES ARE FALLING OFF AND GE AMES 

| | AND WE ARE HUNGRY. WE LOOK 

(THE UNITED STATES) TO DO SOMETHING.* UP TO YoU 

THE FIRST PROJECT TO ASSIST THESE PEOPLE TO ADJUST THEMSELVES was 
STARTED ONLY THIS YEAR. AN AGRICULTURAL PROJECT BUDGETED AT $2.300 
IT IS DESIGNED TO GIVE THE BIKINIANS CROPS OF TARO, AN EDIBLE ROoT,’ 
IT WILL BE AT LEAST A YEAR BEFORE THE PROJECT STARTS PRODUCING Foo). 

USING HAND KNIVES AND THREE AXES, THE MARSHALLESE HAVE CLEARED NINF 
ACRES OF DENSE TROPICAL JUNGLE AS A START. KILI IS A FLAT OVAL ISLAND 
OF ABOUT 120 ACRES, LUSHLY OVERGROWN. 

THESE PEOPLE'S OLD HOME, BIKINI, IS ABOUT 10 TIMES AS BIG--AN ATOLL 
OF 36 ISLANDS SURROUNDING 4 LAGOON ABOUNDING IN FISH. FISHING WAS THE 
PRIMARY OCCUPATION THERE. ON KILI THERE IS NO FISHING SEVEN MONTHS 
OUT OF THE YEAR, WHEN HIGH SURF MAKES IT ALMOST IMPOSSIBLE TO LAND ON 

I AM PROBABLY THE FIRST NEWSPAPERMAN TO VISIT KILI. THE TRIP was 
MADE FROM KWAJALEIN ABOARD A MOTOR VESSEL WIT DR.SAUL RIESENBERG

I AM PROBABLY THE FIRST NEWSPAPERMAN TO VISIT KILI. THE TRIP WAS 
MADE FROM KWAJALEIN ABOARD A MOTOR VESSEL WITH DR«SAUL RIESENBERG 
STAFF ANTHROPOLOGIST FOR THE TRUST TERRITORY} JACK TOBIN, DISTRICT

FIRST TIME AN AMERICAN HAD CALLED AT KILI_IN SIX MONTHS... ... | 

*KING® JUDA » MAGISTRATE AND LEADER OF THE BIKINIANS, SAID HIS 
PEOPLE LEFT 8IKINI MARCH 13, 1946. THEY WERE MOVED TO RONGERIK ATOLL 
FOR TWO YEARS. HUNGER FINALLY DROVE THEM FROM THERE AND THE U.S. 
NAVY PLACED THEM TEMPORARILY IN THE KWAJALEIN ATOLL. MOST OF THEIR 
FIRST DEALINGS WERE WITH THE NAVY WHICH, PRESUMABLY, WAS AN AGENT 
FOR THE ATOMIC ENERGY COMMISSION FORMED IN 1946. IN 1951 THE 
MARSHALLS WERE PLACED UNDER CONTROL OF THE SECRETARY OF THE INTERIOR.

JUDA'S ONLY SHOW OF BITTERNESS WAS IN REFERENCE TO A PAPER A 
NAVAL OFFICER ASKED THEM TO SIGN IN 1951. THE PAPER GAVE AWAY THEIR 
CLAIM TO BIKINI IN EXCHANGE FOR KILI AND THREE SMALL ISLANDS IN THE 
JALUIT ATOLL 30 MILES TO THE NORTHEAST. 

JUDA AND THREE OTHERS FINALLY SIGNED, HE SAID, “BECAUSE THE MAN 
LIED TO US. HE WAS A RASCAL. HE SAID SOMETHING NOT TRUE. HE SATD HE 
WOULD GIVE US ALL SORTS OF PRESENTS BUT HE DID NOT. HE SAID "I WILL 
COME HERE FRIDAY AND GIVE YOU CHICKENS, CLOTHES, AND OTHER THINGS. * 
HE DID NOT COME.®

ONE THING THE BIKINI PEOPLE HAVE NOT LOST IS THEIR ABILITY TO 38E 
FRIENDLY. THE DAY WE ARRIVED THE SEA WAS CALM. EVEN SO, AS WE 
WENT ASHORE IN A SMALL ROWBOAT THE WAVES S3REAKING ON THE CORAL 
POURED OVER THE END OF THE BOAT, WETTING ALL HANDS. WE HAD TO JuMP 
OUT OF THE BOAT IN KNEE DEEP WATER AND WADE ASHORE. AS WE WALKED 
UP A RATHER STEEP BEACH THE NATIVES MET US AND EACH SHOOK HANDS. 

IN FRONT OF THEIR COUNCIL BUILDING WAS A FLAG POLE FROM WHICH THE 
AMERICAN COLORS WERE STREAMED. ON A RISE WAS A THATCHED CHURCH. THE 
PROTESTANT MARSHALLESE ARE DEEPLY RELIGIOUS. THEY FROWN 
ON DRINKING AND SMOKING. THE FRIENDLINESS AND NEATNESS 
OF THE MARSHALLESE ALWAYS IMPRESSES THE VISITOR. THEIR VILLAGE 
HAS WELL MARKED CORAL PATHS. BUILDINGS ARE POOR LOOKING BUT NEAT.

AMERICAN TALK ABOUT "MASSIVE RETALIATION” FRIGHTENED ASTANS BROUGHT ors’ tue ALL THIS TROUBLE IN THE Gorae THE UNITED STATES MUST LOOK OVER 


ACCUSATIONS WASHINGTON THOUGHT IT COULD HELL3OMB AN IDEA OUT 0 
EXISTENCE. AMERICAN TALK OF UNITED ACTION IN ASI4, THE AGREEMENT 
WITH PAKISTAN FOR A MILITARY BASE, AND OTHER AMERICAN MOVES IN ASTA 
BROUGHT ANGER IN THEIR WAKE. ASIANS ACCUSED THE AMERICANS OF 
INVITING THEM TO PROVIDE THE BATTLEGROUND FOR WORLD WAR III. 

BRITAIN WAS ACUTELY CONSCIOUS OF SUCH ASIAN FEELINGS 
PARTICULARLY WITH REGARD TO INDIA AND BURMA, WHERE THE SRITISH, WITH A 
BIG INVESTMENT, RETAINED A GREAT RESERVOIR OF GOODWILL. BRITAIN 
SEEMED HOPEFULLY SEEKING A SOLUTION IN INDOCHINA WHICH WOULD PERMIT 
MARKING TIME. THUS BRITAIN SEEMED DISPOSED TO AGREE TO PARTITION 
WHICH THE AMERICANS OPPOSED. BRITAIN WOULD GO ALONG WITH MEASURES 
TO NEUTRALIZE LAOS AND CAMBODIA, BUT THE AMERICANS WOULD NOT. BRITAIN 
COULD HOPE FOR A NONAGGRESSION PACT INCLUDING THE COMMUNIST NATIONS 
TO END THE THREAT OF WAR. THE UNITED STATES, HOLDING THAT COMMUNIST 
AGREEMENTS HISTORICALLY WERE WORTHLESS, WAS OF NO MIND TO EXTEND 
THAT MUCH RECOGNITION TO RED CHINA. 

LOSING FRIENDS STEADILY IN ASIA, THE UNITED STATES THEN LOST FACE AT 
GENEVA. FAR FROM BEING SAVED, THE FRENCH GOVERNMENT WAS TUMBLED BY 
MOLOTOV+ FAR FROM CONSOLIDATING THE WESTERN ALLIANCE, GENEVA WAS 
GNAWING : 

THE COMMUNISTS MADE HAY. CHOU EN-LAI, RED CHINA'S PREMIER 
TRIPPED OFF TO NEW DELHI TO PUSH A DRIVE’ TO NAIL DOWN ASIAN NEUTRALITY. 
NEVER A MAN TO OVERLOOK AN OPPORTUNITY, HE STOPPED OFF IN CAIRO 
AND TOLD EGYPTIANS HE WAS SYMPATHETIC to THEIR "STRUGGLE FOR 
INDEPENDENCE," MEANING HE FAVORED KICKING THE BRITISH OUT OF THE VITAL 

EZ CANAL ZONE.

BY JOHN SCALI 

WASHINGTON, JUNE 27 (AP)-PRESIDENT EISENHOWER AND PRIME MINISTER 
CHUR CHILL NEARED THE END OF THEIR THREE-DAY CONFERENCE TONIGHT WITH 
NO SIGN OF AGREEMENT ON A JOINT UeSe-BRITISH POLICY FOR SAVING SOUTH- 
EAST ASIA FROM COMMUNISM. 

4 FINAL COMMUNIQUE SUMMING UP THE RESULTS OF THEIR TALKS WAS REPORT- 
ED BEING DRAFTED FOR RELEASE TOMORROW AFTER A LAST MEETING AT THE 
WHITE HOUSE, 

THE ANGLO-AMERICAN LEADERS WERE SAID TO BE STILL SPLIT OVER THE 
NEED FOR QUICK po AS A Ti ha OF FREE NATIONS WHICH 
MIGHT INCLUDE SOME OF TH | e 

‘ EISENHOWER TALKED WITH CHURCHILL FOR AN HOUR IN THE WHITE HOUSE 
STUDY THIS AFTERNOON IN A SEVENTH MEETING ON THE CRITICAL SOUTHEAST 
ASIAN PROBLEM AND EUROPEAN DEFENSE ISSUES. 

LATER, SECRETARY OF STATE DULLES AND A SIX-MAN AMERICAN STAFF MET 

FOR TWO-AND- ONE HALF HOURS WITH BRITISH FOREIGN SECRETARY EDEN TO RE- 


VIEW DETAILS OF THE SAME PROBLEM. 
THE WHITE HOUSE DECLINED TO GIVE ANY INFORMATION EXCEPT THE TOPICS 


DISCUSSED AT THE TWO SESSIONS. 

BUT EDEN ON LEAVING DULLES' HOME TOLD REPORTERS: “GOOD IS MY 
DESCRIPTION OF OUR TALKS. 

BRITISH AMBASSADOR SIR ROGER MAKINS ADDED, “IF MR«EDEN SAYS GOOD, 

HE MEANS IT'S GOOD ALL AROUND.” 

DESPITE THESE REMARKS, INFORMANTS REPORTED CHURCHILL AND EDEN WERE 
CONTINUING TO INSIST THAT NEGOTIATIONS FOR AN ASIATIC ALLIANCE MUST 
BE DELAYED UNTIL FRANCE CONCLUDES HER PRESENT INDOCHINA PEACE TALKS 
WITH THE COMMUNISTS. 

THE FIRST SOLID CLUE ABOUT THE OUTCOME OF THE SESSIONS HERE PROBABLY 
WILL COME TOMORROW NOON (EDT) WHEN CHURCHILL HOLDS A BIG NEWS CONFER- 
ENCE AT A DOWNTOWN HOTEL. 

THE JOINT COMMUNIQUE PROBABLY WILL NOT BE ISSUED UNTIL AFTER CHURCH- 
ILL MEETS WITH THE REPORTERS. IT MAY BE DELAYED UNTIL TUESDAY. 

THE PRESIDENT AND DULLES ARE SCHEDULED TO MEET WITH CHURCHILL AND 
EDEN IN A FAREWELL GET-TOGETHER TOMORROW AT 10 AeMe (EDT). CHURCH 
ILL IS SCHEDULED TO LEAVE TUESDAY AT 3:30 PeMe BY PLANE FOR A BRIEF 
VISIT TO OTTAWA TO TALK WITH CANADIAN GOVERNMENT LEADERS,

(NY) CHICAGOs JUNE 27 -(API-DR«EUGENE, RASINOWITCH, EDITOR OF THE &lt; 
BULLETIN OF THE ATOMIC SCIENTISTS, SAID TODAY THE SECURITY 30ARD DE- 
CISION iN THE OPPENHEIMER CASE *SEEMS, ON THE FACE OF IT, 

ASSURD3; AND, IN ITS IMPLICATIONS, DANGEROUS. 

DRe RABINOWITCH DISCUSSED THE RECENT MAJORITY REPORT IN THE CASE 
OF DR. ROBERT OPPENHEIMER, TOP ATOMIC SCIENTIST, IN AN EDITORIAL IN THE 
CURRENT ISSUE OF THE BULLETIN. 


"THE ABSURDITY,” HE veraee "CONSISTS IN ASSERTING, WITHOUT 


RESERVATION, THAT OPPENHEIMER IS A LOYAL CITIZEN AND HAS HANDLED 
SECRET INFORMATION DISCREETLY IN THE PAST, AND THEN DESCRIBING HIM 
AS A * SECURITY RISK, NOT FIT TO RECEIVE INFORMATION IN THE FuTURE.® 

HE SAID THE MAJORITY REPORT PROVIDES A NEW DEFINITION OF "SECURITY 
RISK’ BY WHICH "A MERE ‘LACK OF ENTHUSIASM’ FOR CERTAIN MILITARY 
POLICIES--EVEN BEFORE THEIR OFFICIAL ADOPTION--CAN BE TAKEN AS 
PRIMA FACIE EVIDENCE OF SUCH "SECURITY RISK'« 

HERE IS WHERE THE DECISION OF THE SECURITY BOARD CEASES To ar 
ABSURD, AND BECOMES HIGHLY DANGEROUS," THE EDITORIAL STATED. 

*IF SUCH A DECISION IS RENDERED INEVITABLE BY THE SECURITY RULES, 
THEN THERE IS SOMETHING FUNDAMENTALLY WRONG WITH THESE RULES.. 

“IT WOULD BE A GRAVE NEW DEPARTURE IN AMERICAN POLITICAL LIFE) 
IF THE ESTABLISHED METHOD OF REPLACING UNPOPULAR OR DISSENTING —”% 
MEMBERS OF THE ADMINISTRATION TEAM SHOULD BECOME THAT OF ACCUSING- ~~ 
THEM AS "SECURITY RISKS'ees IF EVERY CHANGE IN POLICY WERE TO BF 
ACCOMPANIED BY PROCLAMATIONS THAT THE PREVIOUS POLICY MAKERS HAVE 
BEEN NOT MERELY WRONG, BUT DISLOYAL, OR AT LEAST SECURITY RISKS.” 

IN AN ARTICLE IN THE SAME ISSUE, PROF. EDWARD SHILS OF THE 
UNIVERSITY OF CHICAGO, MEMBER OF THE EDITORIAL 30ARD OF THE BULLETIN, 
CALLED THE BOARD REPORT "ONE MORE STEP DOWN THE "SLIPPERY SLOPF® 
WHICH LEADS AWAY FROM A FREE SOCIETYs"e.

(ADVANCE )+eeNEW DELHI, JUNE 28 (AP)-COMMUNIST LEADERS ATTEMPTING To 
SWING ASIAN LABOR BEHIND THEM ARE HAVING TROUBLE SELLING THE MOSCOW- 
PEIPING LINE TO A MILLION AND A HALF INDIAN UNIONIZED WORKERS. 

THE INDIAN WORKER,” MOUTHPIECE OF THE INDIAN NATIONAL TRADE UNION 
CONGRESS, SUGGESTS EDITORIALLY THAT A PEIPING-SPONSORED CONFERENCE OF 
ASIAN WORKERS WAS MORE INTERESTED IN BOOSTING COMMUNISM THAN TRADE UNION 


ISM. 

“THE GATHERING AT PEIPING COULD HAVE RENDERED A POSITIVE $ 
PROMISING CONDITIONS FAVORABLE TO SECURING GAINS BY WORKING MEN Sh ASTAN 
COUNTRIES IN A MANNER WHICH WOULD NEITHER DISLOCATE PECEAFUL ECONOMIC 
PROGRESS NOR TAMPER WITH FREEDOM OR LIBERTY," SAID THE EDITORIAL. 

THE MAGAZINE, WHOSE ROGANIZATION IS RECOGNIZED AS THE LABOR GROUP 

OF THE RULING CONGRESS PARTY IN INDIA, DISCUSSED THE "FRIENDLY DIS- 
CUSSION MEETING OF ASIAN TRADE UNIONS," HELD IN PEIPING IN CONNECTION

BY ROBERT PROSSER ¥ 
TOKYO, JUNE 28 (AP)-NINETY-NINE FRENCHMEN WOUNDED IN INDOCHINA 


FIGHTING2-MANY IN THE LOST BATTLE FOR DIEN BIEN PHU--ARRIVED TODAY IN A 
DRIZZLE; COMPLETING THE SECOND LEG OF A MERCY FLIGHT TO FRANCE IN 
AMERICAN MILITARY PLANES. 
THEY ARE THE FIRST OF FIVE GROUPS OF APPROXIMATELY 100 EACH WHO WILL 
BE EVACUATED FROM INDOCHINA BY THE Us. Se AIR FORCE. 
THEY WALKED OR WERE CARRIED DOWN THE STEEP RAMP OF THE HUGE POT- 
BELLIED C124 GLOBEMASTER THAT BROUGHT THEM FROM INDOCHINA VIA THE 
SOME WAVED STUMPS OF ARMS BLOWN OFF IN THE FIGHTING. 
THIRTY WERE LITTER CASES. 
THE 99 WERE TAKEN TO AN AIR FORCE HOSPITAL NEAR TACHIKAWA AIR 
mage PHERE THREE AE GANDER TARY Chu ST SUERNCEAT AD 
A 
EakeLAnb rEaey WESTOVER, MASS., AND ON TO FRANCE.» 1 ASE» CALIF.

FOUR OTHER PLANE LOADS WILL FOLLOW AT INTERVALS UNTIL MID-JULY 
FRENCH DIGNITARIES FROM THE EMBASSY AND MEMBERS OF THE FRENCH 
COLONY IN TOKYO GATHERED IN THE GRAY MIST TO GREET THE WOUNDED MEN 
SOME LEFT THE PLANE SLOWLY, PAINFULLY, DEPENDING ON CANFS AND 
CRUTCHES TO STEADY THEIR PROGRESS TO AMBULANCES.

Text of Churchill’s Remarks at Press Luncheon in His Honor in Capital 





Following is the text of Prim: 
Minister. Churchill’s intervieu 
with the press in Washington 
yesterday, as recorded by The 
New York Times: 


SIR WINSTON—Mr. Chair- 
man, your Excellencies—I must 
be careful to leave out ‘‘my 
lords,’’ mustn’t I?—ladies and 
gentlemen. I have been here 
now four times, three in the 
war and one since, and I always 
had such very good treatment, 
such a kindly welcome. 

In those occasions we were off 
the record, such absolute, strict 
adherence to the confidence 
which was the basis on which 
we met, that when the Presi- 
dent invited me to come over 
here, I said I would like it to be 
considered whether I shouldn't 
do one of my regular luncheons 
with the Washington Press 
Corps of that one, press corps, 
and so here we are. 

But this time we're off the 
record—on the record, I beg your 
pardon—on the record. I mean 
anything we say may be record- 
ed against us. So if you find me 
full of caution I’m sure you will 
consider that you must be gen- 
erous, as you always are, and 
tender-hearted to an aged guest 
in a critical situation—not criti- 
cal in the sense of immediate 
danger, but one where words, 
carelessly or foolishly used, may 
mak’ it more difficult for ordi- 
nary people to come to a right 
judgment upon the vast events 
through which mankind is mov- 


jng. 
Takes Chance on Question 


I should be very glad to an- 
swer questions. I’ve seen quite 
a lot which have beeg handed 
in, but I'm quite willing to take 
a supplementary, a double sup- 
plementary. I don’t mind. I 
know it’s a serious ordeal, but 
I’ve had a lot of practice in the 
House of Commons and I think 
I'll take a chance on it and so 
anybody can say anything that 
the Chairman allows if he likes. 

ERNEST B. VACCARO, pres- 
ident of the National Press Club 
—I was explaining to Sir Win- 
ston a short while ago that we 
people with television sets in 
this country can understand all 
the English accents because of 
the old movies that we get. 
I’m not too sure that he will 
understand my accent, because 
so few Southern movies get to 
England on television. 

Sir Winston, the first question 
deals with your conference in 
Quebec with Mr. Roosevelt. In 
your communiqué, you spoke of 
“blazing friendship’’ between 
our two countires. What is the 
temperature of that friendship 


aew? 
SIR WINSTON—What is the 








ture of that friendship 
now? Normal! 


a ee 


| pact in Southeast Asia? 


MR. VACCARO—We have two |! 


questions dealing with today’s 
communiqué. The communiqué 
says Britain and the United 
States ‘‘will press forward’’ with 
plans for collective defense in 
Southeast Asia. Do you expect 
formal negotiations for such a 
defense will begin before or 
after the French conclude truce 
talks on Indochina?' The other 


is, does Section 2 on Southeast | 


Asia mean an agreement to | 
gate, a seaside resort in Britain, 


press forward with* defense 
measures without waiting for 
an agreement or lack of it in 
Indochina? 


Cites Joint Statement 


SIR WINSTON—We put out, | 


sir, a statement today, not a 
communiqué. The President and 
I prefer the word “statement” 
in these matters to the well- 
known word ‘‘communiqué.” In 
thig statement, and it is now be- 
ing published and circulated 
throughout the world, the state- 
ment deals with preparing plans 
for dealing with these contin- 
gencies and that work will be 
pressed forward now, imme- 
diately, so that we shall have 
our own ideas ready whether or 
not an agreement is reached on 
Indochina and, of course, if no 
agreement is reached, our ideas 
might well be different from 
what they would be if an agree- 
ment is reached, We are most 
anxious too, that any new situa- 
tion, good or bad, shall con- 
front us after we have prepared 
our minds for what really can- 


not be foreseen with detailed | 


precision beforehand. We have 
agreed to press forward with 
the plans that might be re- 
quired if there were no agree- 
ment and also to consider the 
alternatives, 

MR. VACCARO—The next 
two questions deal with Locar- 
no-type pacts. Is the United 
Kingdom still in favor of the 
Locarno - type nonagegression 
And 


this one goes along with it: 





In fact, I cannot do better 
than read you about it, because 
I think there has been some 
misconception here. It is a very 
great thing to have said a thing 
before; it saves you trouble 
in making up something new 
and also it shows what a far- 
sighted, what a farsighted poli- 
tician you are. You get it both 
ways. Well, now, I had to ad- 
dress my party, the Conserva- 
tive and Unionist party, at Mar- 


in October, last, October the 
10th, and this is what I said 
about Locarno: 

When I spoke about the mas- 
ter thought of Locarno in May, 
I meant of course the plan of 
everybody going against the 
aggressor, whoever it may be, 
and helping the victim, large or 
small. That is no more than the 


United Nations was set up to | 


do, but I said it then, and I say 
it now, it’s a great mistake to 
go and complicate the use of the 
word by dragging in all the 
other connections which that 
word may have which are not 
in any way relevant_{o the pres- 
ent occasion. 
And then I’m ask 

ventive-Hitler magit-t

}} there in a 





What preventive-Hitler magic is 
carno treaty in 
1954 that was not present in Lo- 
carno treaties in 1925? 


SIR WINSTON—There has 


| been a lot of talk about this 


word ‘‘Locarno”’ lately, and I 
think I brought it back to some 
currency when in May of last 
year I referred to the principles 
and to the master thought of 
the Locarno treaty, but that 
doesn’t mean that we have got 
to go and swallow every detail 
of the Locarno treaty concerned 
with totally different facts and 
circumstances at all. 








the Locarno Treaty in 1954 that 
was not present in the Locarno 
Treaty 1925. Well, Arthur Krock 
of The New York Times, a paper 
with which I have had intimate 
and agreeable connections, and 
of which my grandfather was 
once one of the principal pro- 
prietors during your Civil War. 
{Apparently Sir Winston de- 
cided not to follow this line of 
thought.] Well—I—what pre- 
ventive magic is there in the 
Locarno Treaty of 1954 that was 
not present in 1925? Well, Amer- 
ica was not in the Locarno 
Treaty of 1925 and if any treaty 
is to be made now which is to 
have any real—be any real help 
to the bewildered world, I think 
that omission will have to be 
made good. 


Munich Days Recalled 


Certainly a preventive of Hitler 
magic would have been made 
good if we had had the full 
support of your great country in 
the Munich and after-thereafter 
days and I know that such was 
in the mind of the President at 
the time and that he made the 
first moves in that direction. It 
was a very great pity that Mr. 
Eden was not more fully con- 
sulted upon those moves at the 
time. Because I am quite cer- 
tain that half the evils of the 
world can be prevented by our 
going hand-in-hand together 
and, if necessary, standing 
shoulder-to-shoulder. 

MR. VACCARO—What are the 
possibilities of peaceful co-ex- 
istence between Soviet Russia 
and Communist China, on the 
one hand, and non-Communist 
nations on the other? 


a a bn ys 





| tieth century, 


| Man in no country have had a 
| worse time than they have had 











SIR WINSTON—This is a ques- 
tion which raises really, which 
goes really to the heart of the 
matter in current public political 
life. I am of the opinion that | 


| we ought to have a try at 


peaceful co-existence, a real good 
try for it, although anyone can 


| gee that it doesn’t solve all the 


problems, but it may be that 
time, if it is accompanied by 
vigilance, will enable peaceful 
co-existence for a period of 
years to create a very differ- 
ent situation to the one so full | 
of peril, so doomladen as the 
present one under which we live. | 


Feeling of Russian Masses 


I am most anxious that the 
real mood of the people of Rus- 
sia should be known and that 
every opportunity should be 
given for its expression. The 
people of Russia have had a 
very rough time in the twen- 
the century of 
the common man. The common | 


in the twentieth century, with 
all the bloody struggles in which 
they have had to engage, in 
the revolution and the discipli- 
nary measures, internal, inter- 
nal stresses which have fallen 
upon them. 


/ So I am very much in favor of 


tion of what the Russian inten- 





I have a sort of feeling, and 


I may be quite wrong—I feel it 
with my fingertips, that’s all 
I’ve got in the matter—I have a 
sort of feeling that there must 
bea very great wish in Russia 
to have a better time among the 
masses of the people. I think 
they would like to have some of 
the, of the new wealth which 
the times can yield to all peo- 
ples in the world and to the 
masses of people, to have more 
to eat and more various things 
to eat, and more and better 
houses and more knicknacks to 
put in the houses, and would 
like to have more cinemas with 
better programs, and after all, 
democracy, once its material 
needs are met, deserves to have 
some fun. Why shouldn’t they 
have some of the fun, of tele- 
vision which is of various opin- 
ions, but still many like it, 
others have to submit to it. 


Television and football pools | 


—I don’t know if you have them 

here—football pools, but all that 

constitutes the sustaining back- 

ground of the life of the man 
upon whose faithful daily toil 

and exertion, all the progress of 
| society depends. 


So I would like to make quite | 
sure that the Russian people | 
would not feel that they might 
gain far more by a quarter of a 
century of peaceful develop- 
ment in their own country than 
they would by pressing matters 
to a point where we should all 
be led to a situation which 
baffles the human imagination 
in its terror, but which, I am 
quite sure, might leave us, 
would leave us victorious, but 
victorious on a heap of ruins. 


patient, cool, friendly examina- 


tions are. Now, they say, you 
cannot ever believe what they 
say, but I do not think one 
wants to put one’s foundations 
on words. A study of their real 
interests may give a key, a clue 
to their intentions which one 
could trust. 
Wants to Make Sure 


At any rate, I would like to 
make sure, to save the con- 
sciences of the democracdes of 
the world, that no step or stage 
has been n. jected in endeavor- 
ing to test the reality and the 
imminence of our danger. And, 
therefore, I am in favor of try- 
ing to find out where we are 
while at the same time exercis- 
ing the strongest vigilance and 
making it clear—as we are do- 
ing even here, now, 
building—the unbreakable unity 
cf the English-speaking world, 
of Britain and the United States 
in all these affairs. 

You may some day hear that 
T have done something or other 
which looks as if I were going 


to bec Communist, but I 
assure I bave been ail 
ally promi- 
nent peep this. If I 
had been properly supported in 
1919, I think we might have 
strangled bolshevism in its cra- 
dle, but everybody turned up 
their hands and said, “how 
shocking,’’ and I even remem- 
ber making a speech at Fulton 


[Mo.] six years ago at which 


in this , 





I didn’t get a very warm wel- 
come in the United States be- 
cause it was so anti-Russian 
and anti-Communist, but I am 
not. 

But I am not anti-Russian; I 
am violently anti-Communist. 
But I do beg*you to make sure 
that no stone is left unturned 
in this period—to give them a 
chance to grasp the prospects 
of great material well-being 
which will be offered to all 
those millions. And I am rather 
inclined to think, if I had to 
make a prediction, that they 
will not throw away such an 
opportunity. 

But at any rate, as I have 
said, the conscience of the 
Western World will be clear 
only if they are sure that every 
alternative, but a directly hos- 
tile policy, had been searchingly 
examined beforehand. 





| timed moment without 
| hope of producing a definite 





And I am quite clear that the 
fact that we are working to- 
gether and are known to be 
inseparable in our brotherhood 
is going to be one of the factors 
which will give the impression 
of strength without which, I’m 
sure, peace cannot be pre- 
served. 

MR. VACCARO—Have you 
discussed with President Eisen- 
hower your proposal for ‘a 
meeting at the summit’ with 
Premier Malenkov? If so, did 
he agree or not? 


Urged Meeting of Leaders 


SIR WINSTON—Well, you 
know I’m not a bit afraid of 
answering this question. I’m 
not a bit—because I said a year 
and a half ago that I thought 
a meeting of the top leaders 
might be a great help. 


I said it in,the House of Com- 
mons. “AQ: m of that 
opinion. p oygse, the 
time must be“ 2h: the 

ust 


time must be—m rightly 
chosen as it ‘makes a great dif- 
ference to.all the detailed nego- 
tiations which have to take place 


| of the exchange of goods and 





at a modern conference, makes 
a tremendous difference if there | 
is behind it, as it were, the con- 
senting minds of the heads of 
governments and states. 

And, therefore, I think that 
what I said I don’t in the least 
go back from or retire from. At 
the same time, I also said the 
consequences of doing anything 
like that—if it didn’t come off— 
might be to darken the scene, 
might be very serious. 

I am not at all—if I appear 
sometimes to be jocular—I’m not 
at all unaware of the profound 
anxieties and preoccupations 
which hang over so many minds. 
And never in the world before 
has there been such reason for 
human beings to consider their 


fate and their future more di- 


rectly than at the present. 

And, therefore, I say that to 
go without good occasion, to 
hold such a meeting without 


good occasion, and at an ill- 
some 


amelioration of the world situa- 
tion, might be not to help mat- 
ters but to worsen them, and 
not to ease tension, but to 
sharpen it. 

Therefore, you won’t expect 
me to give you any definite 
answer upon this subject today, 
but I have not altered any of 
my views, and I shall continue 
to put them forward in suitable 
ways at suitable times and z 
earnestly hope that nothing 
will be neglected by any of 
the Western countries which can 
make for comprehension, better 





comprehension... of Russia than!) 


what we have at the present 
time. 


It doesn’t follow-at all that —— 
the men in the Kremlin will 
always be able to be largely in- 
dependent of the opinion of the 
vast Russian masses. I think 
not, but I’m quite sure that 
nothing is more likely to bring 
about a modification of the 
rigorous Russian system of com- 
munism than contacts between ™uch of their own philosophy 
the Russian people and the —~— 
peoples of the Western world, and th

TO END THE SEVEN-YEAR OLD WAR.. 




















| Recalls His Visits to U. S. 


/ deen over here in the war, | 
and since the war, and before 
the war, in important govern- 
ment missions a good many 
"i times and never have found a 
more friendly, more tolerant 
atmosphere and as for my rela- 
tiéns with your, President, they 
| have lasted twelve years of war 
and peace and never have I felt 
them more high and comforting 
than at the present time. I feel 
that I have a strong personal 
friend with whom I can discuss 
matters absolutely on the dead 

level and I rejoice that, if I'm 
not intruding on party politics, 
I rejoice that General Eisen- 
hower is at the head of your 
country at a time when your 
country has Been called upon to 
‘become the head of the world. 
| MR. VACCARO—Mr. Prime 
Minister, I cquid ask these ques- 
tions all afternoon. I personally 
never had such a big house, but 
I do know that you have a 
schedule, so I'll ask this final 
question: In view of the impor- 
tance of strengthening the Mid- 
die East against Soviet expan- 
sion, do you see any prospects 
or Arab-Israel peace? 

SIR WINSTON-—I am a Zion- 
ist. Let me make that clear. I 
was one of the original ones, 
| after the Balfour Declaration, 

and I have worked faithfully 
for it. [The Balfour Declaration 
in 1917 favored the establish- 
ment in Palestine of a national 
home for the Jewish people. ] 

1 think it a most wonderful 
thing that this community 
should have established itself so 
effectively, turning the desert 
into fertile gardens and thriving 
townships and should have af- 
forded a refuge to millions of 
their co-religionists who had 
suffered so fearfully under the 
Hitler and not only the Hit- 
jer, persecution. I think it's a 
wonderful thing. 

I also bashfully have great 
liking for the Arabs. I was the 
man who appointed Abdullah to 
be King of Transjordania. I 
presented to the Cabinet that 
jdea, and they put it into force. 
Last year he’s been murdered. 


But I have the greatest regard 
for the Arabs, too. Really, I 
think we ought to be able, some- 
how or other, to use our influ- 
ence from every quarter to 
make them work out a plan 
together. 

It’s so easy to reduce what 
little there is of that part of 
the world to the desert, to the 
level of the desert, and that is 
| all that could be done by their 
quarrels, whereas by agree- 
ments and so forth, we may be 
able to greatly increase the 
population who can live in that 
area—there were far more 2,000 
years ago than there are now 
in Palestine—and we not only 
can greatly increase the pop- 
ulation in that area, but we can 
bring a harmony there which 
will be a security and not an 
element of anxiety in the Middle 
East. Let us be tolerant and 
let us be friendly and let us 
support Arab rights and help 
eerwara legitimate Zionist as- 
rations, [Applause]. , 











Let me ask you—and thank 
you for that really grand ova- 
tion, which I intensely enjoyed 
—let me ask you to give one 
more good cheer for my faith- 
ful friend and lieutenant, the 
Foreign Secretary, Mr. Anthony 
Eden. [Applause]. Thank you.

WASHINGTON, June 28 (P)— - 
The text of the statement issued . 
today by President Hisenhower - 
and Prime Minister Churchill fol- 


lows: 


In these few days of friendly 
and fruitful conversations, we 
have considered various sub- 
jects of mutual and world in- 


terest. 
I—Western Europe 


We are agreed that the Ger- 
man Federal Republic should 
take its place as an equal part- 
ner in the community of West- 
ern nations, where it can make 
its proper contribution to the 
defense of the free world. We 
are determined to achieve this 
goal, convinced that the Bonn 
and Paris treaties provide the 
best way. We welcome the re- 
cent statement by the French 
Prime Minister that an end 
must be put to the present un- 
certainties. 

The European Defense Com- 
munity Treaty has been ratified 
by four of the six signatory 
nations, after exhaustive de- 
bates over a period of more than 





IlI—Atomic Matters 


We also discussed technical 
cooperation on atomic energy. 
We agreed that both our coun- 
tries would benefit from such 
cooperation to the fullest extent 
allowed by United States legis- 


In addition to these Specific 
matters, we discussed the basic 
principles underlying the policy 
of our two countries. An agreed 
declaration setting forth cer- 
tain of these will be made avail- 
able tomorrow. 











two years. Naturally these na- 
tions are unwilling to disregard 
their previous legislative approv- 
als or to reopen these complex 
questions. 

In connection with these trea- 
ties, the United States and the 
United Kingdom have given im- 
portant assurances, including 
the disposition of their armed 
forces in Europe, in order to 
demonstrate their confidence in 
the North Atlantic community 
and in the E, D. ©, and the 
Bonn treaties. 

It is our conviction that fur- 
there delay in the entry into 
force of the E. D. C. and Bonn 
treaties Would damage the soli- 
darity of the Atlantic nations. 

We wish to reaffirm that the 
program for European unity in- 
spired by France, of which the 
E. D. C. is only one element, so 
promising to peace and pros- 
perity in Europe, continues to 
have our firm support. 


Ii—Southeast Asia 


We discussed Southeast Asia 
and, in particular, examined the 
situation which would arise from 
the conclusion of an agreement 


LT TT. 


o_O 


on Indochina. We also consid- 


ered the situation which would 
follow from failure to reach 
such an agreement.

LOT OOO SLY? JUNE 29 (AP)--VIET NAM'S NEW PREMIER NGO DINH DIEM 


ISSCHEDULED TO FLY HERE TOMORROW FOR CONFERENCES WITH NORTHERN VIBTNOM- 
SSE OFFICIALS WHO FEAR THE FRENCH ARE BEGINNING TO PULL OUT OF SOME. 
IMPORTANT SECTORS OF THE VITAL RED RIVER DELTA TRIANGLE. 

THE PREMIER IS SLATED TO MEET WITH NGUYEN TRI, GOVERNOR OF NORT 
VIET NAM, WHO TOLD NEWSMEN TODAY HE HAD CALLED FOR THE DISCUSSIONS 
BECAUSE 940,000 TO 50,000 HAVE BEEN EVACUATED FROM FOUR CITIES IN THE 
SOUTHERN_PART OF THE BELTA IN THE PAST TWO WEEKS." 

ALTHOUGH HE DID NOT SPECIFY WHO THE EVACUEES WERE, OTHER VIETNAMESE 
SOURCES SAID THEY WERE FRENCH CIVILIANS AND FAMILIES OF VIETNAMESE 
SOLDIERS FIGHTING WITH THE FRENCH UNION FORCES AGAINST THE COMMUNIST- 
ED VIETMINH. 
be TRI SAID THE NORTHERN VIETNAMESE OFFICIALS FEAR A FRENCH WITHDRAWAL 
FROM THE DELTA WOULD ABANDON FOUR MILLION PEOPLE TO COMMUNISM." 

HANOI, NORTHERN VIETNAMESE CAPITAL, BUZZED WITH RUMORS THAT MAJOR 
UNITS ARE IN THE PROCESS OF BEING WITHDRAWN FROM KEY DEFENSE BASTIONS 
AT NINH BINH, PHAT DIEM AND NAM DINH--RANGING FROM 60 MILES SOUTH To 70 
MILES SOUTHEAST OF HANOI. 

FRENCH OFFICIALS HAVE SPOKEN GUARDEDLY FOR THE PAST TWO WEEKS OF 
®DELTA DEFENSE REORGANIZATION® AND "TIGHTENED PERIMETER OF DEFENSE." 
THEY HAVE INSISTED ALL ALONG, HOWEVER, THERE HAS BEEN NO REAL EVACUATION 
OF ANY PORTION OF THE DELTA. THEY HAVE OFFICIALLY DENIED REPORTS OF 
MAJOR WITHDRAWALS AT THE THREE BASTIONS.} 

AT FRENCH ARMY BRIEFINGS, SPOKESMEN HAVE MENTIONED ONLY THE EVACUATION 
AND DESTRUCTION OF SMALL FOR 
FAST OF HANOL. SUCH WITHDRAWALS ARE IN LINE WITH A PREVIOUSLY ANNOUNCED 
FRENCH PLAN TO “TIGHTEN” THE TRIANGLE OF DEFENSE AROUND THE RICE- 
eTCH DELTA, THE WAR CAPITAL OF HANOI AND THE VITAL RAIL AND ROAD SUPPLY 
CINE BETWEEN HANOL AND THE SEAPORT OF HAIPHONG WHERE AMERICAN WAR 
SUPPLIES ARE BROUGHT IN. 

THE FRENCH HAVE GIVEN ASSURANCES TO VIETNAMESE OFFICIALS THAT SUCH 
eEORGANIZATION OF THE DELTA DEFENSES DOES NOT MEAN THE FRENCH ARE 
MSANDONING ANY PORTION OF THE DELTA. 

SUT THE VIETNAMESE FEARS ARE INTENSIFIED BY THE UNCERTAINTY 
oF WHAT I$ HAPPENING IN GENEVA. THERE IS A GROWING BELIEF AMONG THE 
JEETNAMESE OFFICIALS IN HANOI THAT THE FRENCH NEGOTIATORS AT GENEVA 
“AY MAKE A CEASE FIRE AGREEMENT THAT WILL, IN EFFECT, CEDE THE BULK OF

EUGENE MeZUCKERT--wAS ASSISTANT SECRETARY OF THE AIR FORCE WHEN HE 
WAS APPOINTED TO THE COMMISSION IN 1952. A LAWYER, HE WAS WITH THE 
SECURITIES EXCHANGE COMMISSION FOR THREE YEARS--1937-40 AND BECAME 
A TEACHER AND ADMINISTRATOR AT HARVARD UNIVERSITY, WHERE DURING 
WORLD WAR IL HE ALSO SERVED AS CONSULTANT TO THE ARMY AIR FORCE AND 
INSTRUCTION OF AN AIR FORCE SCHOOL CONDUCTED AT THE UNIVERSITY. 

JOSEPH CAMPBELL--LEFT THE VICE-PRESIDENCY OF COLUMBIA UNIVERSITY TO 
ACCEPT THE COMMISSION POST IN JULY OF LAST YEAR. AN ACCOUNTANT, HE 
HAS BEEN WITH VARIOUS FINANCIAL AND REAL ESTATE ORGANIZATIONS IN 


ADDITION TO SERVING IN MANY EXECUTIVE ROLES AT COLUMBIA.

WASHINGTON, JUNE 29 (AP)-A PACIFIC AIR FORCE IS BEING FORMED TO TAKE 
COMMAND OVER ALL UNITED STATES AIR FORCE INSTALLATIONS AND PERSONNEL IN 
THE VAST OCEAN AREA FROM HAWAIE TO THE BOUNDARIES OF THE FAR EAST 

a 

COMMANDING THE PACIFIC AIR FORCE, WHICH IS TO BECOME OPERATIVE NEXT 
MONTH, WILL BE MAJ. GEN. SORY SMITH, NOW DIRECTOR OF PUBLIC RELATIONS 
FOR THE AIR FORCE.

SINCE WORLD WAR Ile DURING THE WAR, COMBAT AND TRANSPORT OPERATIONS I!

MOSCOW, JUNE 30 (AP)--THE SOVIET COUNCIL OF MINISTERS ANNOUNCED TO- 
NIGHT RUSSIA HAS OPENED WHAT THEY DESCRIBED AS THE WORLD'S FIRST POWEF 


STATION RUN BY ATOMIC ENERGY. : 
--DASH-- 


A MOSCOW RADIO BROADCAST HEARD IN LONDON SAID A SMALL ATOMIC~ENGINE 
POWER STATION HAD BEEN OPENED AND SOVIET AUTHORITIES AND ENGINEERS ARE 
PLANNING CONSTRUCTION OF OTHER PLANTS WITH BIGGER CAPACITIES FROM 
atatt TO Mg Fes KILOWATTS. THE BROADCAST GAVE NO HINT AS TO THE 


ATION OF THE STATION. 
BOTH THE UNITED STATES AND BRITAIN HAVE STARTED WORK ON ATOMIC 


OLANTS TO GENERATE ELECTRICITY FOR INDUSTRY BUT NEITHER IS SCHEDULED 
RN COMPLETION FOR SEVERAL YEARS. SEVERAL OTHER NATIONS, INCLUDING 
FRANCE AND CANADA, HAVE ANNOUNCED PLANS FOR SIMILAR PROJECTS. 

THE MOSCOW BROADCAST QUOTED A STATEMENT FROM THE SOVIET COUNCIL 
OF MINISTERS AS SAYING THE NEW RUSSIAN INSTALLATION, WITH A CAPACITY 
OF 5.000 KILOWATTS, WENT INTO OPERATION SUNDAY PRODUCING ELECTRICAL 
CURRENT FOR BOTH INDUSTRY AND AGRICULTURE. 

IN NEV YORK, ELECTRICAL INDUSTRY SPOKESMAN SAID A 5)000-«ILOvATT 
PLANT IS SMALL’ BY UsS. STANDARDS. THEY ESTIMATED THE OUTPUT OF SUCH 
\ STATION WOULD FILL THE ELECTRICITY NEEDS OF ONLY ABOUT 5,000 AVERAGE 
AMERICAN HOUSEHOLDS, OR LIGHT ABOUT 335 DISPLAYS LIKE THE AUGE TORCH 
\TOP THE STATUE OF LISERTY IN NEW YORK HARBORs 

UeSe ELECTRICAL COMPANIES NOW ARE BUILDING EQUIPMENT FOR 
MMERICA'S FIRST INDUSTRIAL POWER PLANT, TO BE LOCATED AT SHIPPINGPORT, 

: OHIO RIVER. 
Phe OMOKESMAN FOR THE UsSs ATOMIC ENERGY COMMISSION IN NEW YORK SAID 
THIS 35-MILLION DOLLAR STATION WILL HAVE 4 CAPACITY OF 60,000 KILOWATT 
pei TIMES THE CAPACITY CLAIMED FOR THE RUSSIAN PLANT--BUT STILL IS 
CONSIDERED LARGELY AN EXPERIMENTAL MODEL. 
CON N WASHINGTON, HOWEVER, REP. We STERLING COLE (R-NY) SAID THE 
SOVIET ANNOUNCEMENT UNDERLINED THE NEED FOR A NEW ATOMIC ENERGY B8ILL 
APENING THE WAY FOR PRIVATE INDUSTRY TO DEVELOP PEACETIME POWER.” 

ASSERTING THE SHIPPINGPORT PLANT WILL NOT BE COMPLETED UNTIL

UP NEW DEFENSE WORKS AROUND HAIPHONG. THE PORT WILL BE THE SITE oF 
THEIR BEACHHEAD STAND AGAINST AN ESTIMATED EIGHT REBEL DIVISIONS--AND 
100,000 OTHER TROOPS LONG OPERATING INSIDE THE DELT 
ARE EXPECTED TO UNLEASH IN THE BATTLE FOR THE DELTA

WASHINGTON, JUNE 30 CAP)-PRESIDENT ELSENHOWER SAID TODAY THE 
GOVERNMENT WILL ENTERTAIN AN APPEAL BY Je ROBERT OPPENHEIMER IF 
THE ATOMIC PIONEER DECIDES To TRY FOR REVERSAL OF THE 4-1 VOTE 
REFUSING HIM SECURITY CLEARANCE. 

THE ATOMIC ENERGY COMMISSION YESTERDAY FOUND THAT OPPENHEIMER IS 
UNFIT TO HANDLE AMERICA'S NUCLEAR SECRETS BECAUSE OF “DEFECTS OF 
CHARACTER se «eAND DANGEROUS ASSOCIATIONS." THAT DECISION CLOSED OUT A 
(ONG AND WORDY LEGAL BATTLE INVOLVING THE 50-YEAR-OLD PHYSICIST. 

FISENHOWER, ON WHOSE ORDERS OPPENHEIMER'S SECURITY CLEARANCE WAS 
SUSPENDED LAST DECEMBER, REFUSED AT HIS NEWS CONFERENCE TODAY To 
COMMENT ON THE AEC DECISION.

! NE 30 (AP)-THE UNITED STATES, AUSTRALIA, AND NEW ZEA 
ae ED TODAY FOR *IMMEDIATE ACTION" TO PSTABLISH A COLLECTIVE 


DEFENSE ARRANGEMENT IN COMMUNIST-THREATENED SOUTHEAST ASIA. . 


THE THREE GOVERNMENTS JOINTLY ISSUED THIS CALL AFTER A MEETING OF 
SECRETARY OF STATE DULLES, AUSTRALIAN FOREIGN MINISTER RICHARD G. 
CASEY AND NEW ZEALAND AMBASSADOR LESLIE Ke MUNRO. 

THE STATE DEPARTMENT SAID "CONTINUING CONSULTATIONS” WILL Go ON 
AMONG THE THREE GOVERNMENTS TO HELP SPEED ACTION IN BRINGING ABOUT 
THE ANTI-COMMUNIST ALLIANCE DULLES HAS 3EEN URGING THE LAST THREE 
MONTHS. THAN EF reta wp 

THE STATEMENT, APPROVED BY THE THREE NATIONS,APPEARED TO PUT 
AUSTRALIA AND NEW ZEALAND ON RECORD AS MORE EAGERNFOR SWIFT ACTION TO 
BLOCK THE COMMUNIST ADVANCE IN SOUTHEAST ASIA. 

THE STATEMENT ISSUED AFTER PRESIDENT EISENHOWER AND PRIME MINISTER 
CHURCHILL ENDED THEIR FIVE-DAY CONFERENCE TUESDAY 

SAID MERELY THAT THE TWO GOVERNMENTS WOULD "PRESS FORWARD WITH PLANS 
FOR. COLLECTIVE DEFENSE."

DAY APPROVED. &amp; BROAD NEW BILL PERMITTING EXCHANGE

THE COMMITTEE COMPLETED WEEKS OF CONSIDERATION WITH FINAL ACTION AT 
, CLOSED DOOR MEETING THIS AFTERNOON. THE VOTE WAS UNANIMOUS, ALTHOUGH 
Some MEMBERS REPORTEDLY RESERVED THE RIGHT TO OPPOSE PORTIONS OF THE 


MEASURE ON THE FLOOR. 
SEP. We STERLING COLE (R-NY), THE CHAIRMAN, SAID HE WOULD PRESS FOR 
IMMEDIATE CONGRESSIONAL ACTION ON THE MEASURE, DESIGNED TO CARRY OUT ° 
PRESIDENT EISENHOWER'S ATOMIC PROGRAM. 

THE MEASURE WAS COMPLETED SHORTLY AFTER MOSCOW RADIO CLAIMED RUSSIA 
HAD COMPLETED AND PUT IN OPERATION THE WORLD'S FIRST INDUSTRIAL POWER 
STATION USING ATOMIC ENERGY, WITH A CAPACITY OF 5,000 KILOWATTS. 

THE FIRST COMMERCIAL AMERICAN STATION, BEING BUILT FOR THE GOVERN- 
MENT NEAR PITTSBURGH, IS DUE FOR COMPLETION IN 1957. 

IN VIEW OF THE MOSCOW ANNOUNCEMENT, COLE SAID THAT FAILURE OF CON- 
GRESS TO ACT ON THE MEASURE "WOULD MEAN DELIBERATELY EXPOSING OURSELVES 
TO AN INFERIOR POSITION IN THE WORLD PRODUCTION OF INDUSTRIAL POWER.” 

ONE SECTION OF THE SILL IS DESIGNED TO STIMULATE THE DEVELOPMENT OF 
CIVILIAN NUCLEAR POWER BY PERMITTING PRIVATE ENTERPRISE TO OPERATE 
ATOMIC FACILITIES, UNDER 4 MODIFIED SYSTEM OF PATENT RIGHTS. 

THE MEASURE AL$0 WOULD AUTHORIZE THE PRESIDENT TO FURNISH ALLIES 
WITH INFORMATION ON THE EXTERNAL CHARACTERISTICS OF NUCLEAR WEAPONS, 
THELR PERFORMANCE AND METHODS OF BUILDING DEFENSES AGAINST THEM. THE 
PRESENT LAW BARS EXCHANGE OF THIS DATA. 

THIS AUTHORITY, DESCRIBED AS THE HEART OF THE PRESIDENT'S “NEW LOOK" 
AT EUROPEAN DEFENSES, WAS DISCUSSED AT LENGTH IN LAST WEEK'S EISENHOW- 
ER-CHURCHILL TALKS.

WASHINGTON, JUNE 30 (AP)-THE HOUSE JUDICIARY COMMITTEE TODAY APPROVED 
4 BILL PROVIDING THE DEATH PENALTY FOR PEACETIME ESPIONAGE, AND REQUIR- 
ING THE REGISTRATION OF FOREIGN SASOTEURS AND SPIES. 

THE BILL, WHICH ALSO REDEFINES AND BROADENS THE DEFINITION OF SA80- 
TAGE, IS PART OF THE LEGISLATIVE PROGRAM SENT TO CONGRESS BY ATTY. 

GEN. BROWNELL FOR DEALING WITH COMMUNISTS AND SUBVERSIVES.

TION AS EVIDENCE IN CRIMINAL PROSECUTIONS INVOLVING THE NATIONAL SECUR- 
[TY. IT ALSO IS STUDYING OTHER ADMINISTRATION PROPOSALS INCLUDING LOSS 
OF AMERICAN CITIZENSHIP FOR PERSONS CONVICTED OF TREASON.

WASHINGTON,JUNE 30 (AP)-THE ARMY'S INTELLIGENCE DIVISION IS CHANGING 
\ CIRCULAR WHICH “PROHIBITED” NEWSPAPERS AND OTHER NEWS MEDIA FROM USING 
CERTAIN MATERIAL WHICH THE DIVISION DECIDED WAS FOR “OFFICIAL USE ONLY." 

AN ARMY CIRCULAR DATED JUNE 10 AND DEALING WITH THE SAFEGUARDING 
OF IDENTITY OF COUNTER INTELLIGENCE CORPS PERSONNEL HAD INVOKED THE 
JEW "FOR OFFICIAL USE ONLY” METHOD OF RETAINING CONTROL OF INFORMATION. 
IT SAID THAT STEMP WOULD 3E USED ON “OFFICIAL INFORMATION WHICH DOES NoT 
REQUIRE CLASSIFICATION IN THE INTERESTS OF NATIONAL DEFENSE.® 

THEN THE CIRCULAR PROCEEDED TO SPECIFY THAT "DISCLOSURE OF INFORMA- 
TION IN THIS CATEGORY IN PUSLIC NEWS MEDIA IS PROHIBITED.® 

OTHER ARMY OFFICIALS, LEARNING OF THE NEW ORDER, POINTED OUT TO THE 
INTELLIGENCE DIVISION THAT THE ARMY HAS NO AUTHORITY To "PROHIBIT" 
PUBLICATION OF INFORMATION WHICH DOES NOT FALL WITHIN THE TERMS OF THE 
ESPIONAGE ACT OR OTHER FEDERAL LAW. THE ESPIONAGE ACT DEALS WITH 
DISCLOSURE OF INFORMATION INVOLVING ACTUAL MILITARY SECURITY-- 
“CLASSIFICATION IN THE INTEREST OF NATIONAL DEFENSE." 

| DME! HE CIRCULAR NOW BEING PREPARED DELETES THE 
REFERENCE TO THE PROHIBITION UPON NEWS MEDIA. 

SUBSTITUTED FOR THE SECTION IS ONE SPECIFYING ONLY THAT INFORMATION 
REVEALING THE IDENTITY OF INDIVIDUALS ASSIGNED TO COUNTER INTELLIGENCE 
WORK "WHICH REQUIRES SPECIAL PROTECTION IN THE INTEREST OF INTELLIGENCE 
OPERATIONS" WILL BEAR THE FOR-OFFICIAL-USE-ONLY STAMP. , 

AN ARMY SPOKESMAN SAID A LATER, OVERALL ORDER PROVIDING FOR USE oF 
THE NEW CLASSIFICATION WILL NOT {NCLUDE ANY LANGUAGE LIKE THAT USED IN 
THE INTELLIGENCE DIVISION'S ORIGINAL ORDER. 

THE OFFICIAL=USE-ONLY CLASSIFICATION IS COMING INTO USE IN VARIOUS 
GOVERNMENT AGENCIES, IN ADDITION TO THE MILITARY DEPARTMENT. IT 
PROVIDES AUTHORITY FoR CLASSIFYING MATERIAL NOT COVERED BY MILITARY 
SECURITY LAWS AND REGULATIONS, INCLUDING MATTERS RELATING TO POLICY 


AND ADMINISTRATIVE PLANNINGS)

\PPEARS EVERY 10 DAYS IN 400,000 COPIES. THAT I$ NOT "MUCH FOR A

OF THE 4AGAZINE MUST BE THE UPPER LAYER OF THE PARTY AND GOVERNMENT 
3UREAUCRACY--AT WHOM MUCH OF ITS SATIRE IS AIMED. KROKODIL I$ 
PUBLISHED IN COLOR. IT USES THE WORK OF SOME OF THE FINEST ARTISTS

ANNAPOLIS, MDey JUNE 30-(AP)-VICE ADM.s CHARLES TURNER JOY TOMORROW 
STEPS OUT OF A 4O-YEAR NAVY CAREER WHICH ONCE THRUST HIM INTO THE ROLE 
OF CHIEF NESsTi ATOR WITH THE COMMUNISTS IN KOREA. 

NOW THAT HE HAS BEEN SUPERINTENDENT OF THE NAVAL ACAADEMY FOR TwO 
YEARS, FOLLOWING HARD ON 11 FRUITLESS MONTHS 4S HEAD OF THE UNITED 
NATIONS TRUCE NEGOTIATING TEAM IN KOREA, HE SAYS? 

THE QUALITIES OF PATIENCE AND FIRMNESS ARE UNIVERSAL IN THEIR 
APPLICATION. THEY ARE JUST AS USEFUL IN RUNNING A NAVAL ACADEMY AS THE 
ARE NECESSARY IN NEGOTIATING WITH THE COMMUNISTS. 

"HERE YOU HAVE A CONFERENCE AND EVERYONE ARRIVES AT A DEFINITE AND 
AMICABLE CONCLUSION IN A SHORT TIME. YOU CAN'T NEGOTIATE WITH THE 
COMMUNISTS UNLESS YOU NEGOTIATE FROM 4 POSITION OF MILITARY STRENGTH. 

"WE MUST TURN OUT LEADERS HERE AND WE MUST UNDERSTAND THE THINKING 
AND PSYCHOLOGY OF OUR ENEMY. I HAVE TRIED TO GET LECTURERS HERE WHO 
ARE AUTHORITIES ON WHAT MAKES COMMUNISTS TICK." 

SOME OF THE NAVY'S HIGHEST RANKING OFFICERS ARE COMING HERE 
FOR THE 11 A.M. (EDT) RETIREMENT CEREMONIES. REAR ADM. WALTER F. 
BOONE, NAVAL AVIATOR, WILL SUCCEED JOY. 

THE 59-YEAR-OLD NATIVE OF ST. LOUIS IS NOT EXACTLY TAKING LEAVE OF 
THE NAVY WHEN HE GOES ON INACTIVE STATUS FOR MEDICAL REASONS. 

FROM HIS HOME AT LAJOLLA, CALIF., JOY HOPES TO SPARK A DRIVE FOR 
FUNDS TO BUILD A NEW WAR MEMORIAL STADIUM AT THE ACADEMY. 

THE ADMIRAL IS AN AVID SPORTS FAN. HE BELIEVES PHYSICAL FITNESS IS 
A VITAL PART OF ACADEMY TRAINING. SO HE HAS BEEN CONCERNED THAT THE 
INDOOR SPACE FOR ATHLETICS AT "THE YARD" IS VERY LITTLE MORE THAN WHEN 
HE WAS A MIDSHIPMAN, CLASS OF 1916. 

SINCE HE CAME HERE HE HAS PATIENTLY BUT FIRMLY PRESSED FOR A NEW 
GYMNASIUM. CONGRESS HAS AUTHORIZED IT. THE NEW STADIUM IS DIFFERENT. 
THERE ARE NO FEDERAL FUNDS FOR A STADIUM.

1y-MOSCOW RADIO HINTED TODAY RED CHINA MAY 
RMALIZE" DIPLOMATIC RELATICNS WITH BRITAIN 
IF THE LATTER JOINS A PROPOSED SOUTHEAST ASIA DEFENSE BLOC AGAINST 
COMMUNISM. 

BRITAIN GAVE DIPLOMATIC RECOGNITION TO THE PEIPING REGIME 4 1/2 
YEARS AGO AND HAS SINCE HAD A CHARGE D'AFFALRES IN PEIPINGe NO 
SIMILAR RED CHINA DIPLOMAT HAS BEEN STATIONED HERE BUT THE. COM-

SOON UNDER A CHARGE D*AFFAIRES. 

THE MOSCOW RADIO BROADCAST WAS AN ENGLISH LANGUAGE COMMENTARY 
ON THE EISENHOWER-CHURCHILL TALKS IN WASHINGTON. IT NOTED THAT 
Hy BESCUSSE ED THE SOUTHEAST ASIA COLLECTIVE DEFENSE BLOC AND 
DECLARED: 

fo BRITAIN’S REAL STAND IS ON THIS QUESTION» THE JOINT 
STATEMENT OF EISENHOWER AND CHURCHILL DOES NOT AKE CLEAR. 
NOR rs THAT ACCIDENTAL. 

"FOR BRITAIN'S PARTICIPATION IN THE KIND OF SLOC WHICH THE UeSAe 
IS PLANNING, WOULD NOT ONLY DAMAGE THE NORMALIZATION OF RELATIONS 
WITH THE CHINESE PEOPLE'S REPUSLIC BUT WOULD ALSO DAMAGE RELATIONS 
WITH MEMBER COUNTRIES OF THE BRITISH COMMONWEALTH. 

“THIS WOULD BE FIRST AND FOREMOST TRUE OF RELATIONS WITH INDIA, 
WHICH STANDS FOR PEACE AND SECURITY IN ASIA AND NOT FOR 
AGGRESSIVE BLOCS THREATENING TO CRUSH THE INDEPENDENCE AND LIBERTIES 
OF ASIAN COUNTRIES."
